Small breweries often choose to either use raw local water or tweak mineral levels to suit a particular beer style. Even subtle differences in pH or mineral balance can emphasise bitterness, sweetness, or mouthfeel.\n\n3. **Yeast Strains:** Yeast does far more than just ferment sugars into alcohol — it creates esters and phenols that form much of beer’s aroma and flavour. Two breweries may use entirely different yeast strains for the same style, resulting in wildly different profiles: one batch might lean fruity or tropical, another more spicy or earthy.\n\n4. **Brewing Technique and Equipment:** Small differences in mash temperature, fermentation temperature, oxygen exposure, and filtration can alter flavour complexity and clarity. Craftspeople often have their own methodology or 'signature processes' — essentially a brewer’s fingerprint.\n\n5. **Batch Size and Freshness:** Small-batch brewing means more variability between releases, which can be part of its charm. Beers at a local tasting event are often consumed very fresh, intensifying hop aromas or other volatile flavour compounds that might fade in mass-produced equivalents.\n\n6. **Creative Additions:** Especially in BC’s craft scene, brewers experiment. Two versions of a pale ale could diverge simply because one uses locally foraged spruce tips or seasonal fruit during secondary fermentation.\n\nFrom a flavour science standpoint, it’s a mix of biology (yeast behaviour), chemistry (water and malt chemistry), and artistry (ingredient choice and brewing style). This temporal aspect became foundational in environmental monitoring, an approach still used in modern GIS (Geographic Information Systems) workflows.\n\nDuring World War II, the field expanded with the integration of stereoscopic photography — overlapping images viewed through stereoscopes allowed analysts to perceive depth and measure terrain elevations more accurately. Let’s unpack both terms and then compare them in real-world scenarios.\n\n**1. What is Edge AI?**\nEdge AI means running artificial intelligence algorithms *locally* on hardware devices — right at the ‘edge’ of the network, close to where the data is created. This could mean on a smartphone, IoT device, security camera, or embedded sensor. The device itself has enough computing power (often using specialized chips like TPUs or low-power GPUs) to analyze data without sending it to a remote server.\n\n**2. What is Cloud AI?**\nCloud AI, on the other hand, involves sending raw data over the internet to powerful remote servers for processing. The heavy computation happens in large-scale data centers, and the processed output is sent back to the device.\n\n---\n\n**Key differences in practice:**\n\n- **Latency:**\n  - Edge AI: Extremely low latency because processing happens locally; great for real-time reactions (like object detection in autonomous drones).\n  - Cloud AI: Higher latency because data must travel to a remote server and back.\n\n- **Connectivity Requirements:**\n  - Edge AI: Can work offline once the model is deployed.\n  - Cloud AI: Requires a stable internet connection.\n\n- **Data Privacy:**\n  - Edge AI: Keeps sensitive raw data on the device, enhancing privacy.\n  - Cloud AI: Sends raw data to the cloud, which raises privacy and compliance considerations.\n\n- **Compute Resources:**\n  - Edge AI: Limited by the device’s hardware capacity.\n  - Cloud AI: Access to virtually unlimited processing power.\n\n---\n\n**Example scenario:**\nLet’s say you were building a photo-tagging feature for a social media app:\n- **Edge AI version:** The phone processes the image directly using a compact AI model, tags the people, and shows results instantly without an internet connection.\n- **Cloud AI version:** The phone uploads the image to a server, the server runs a large and more accurate AI model, and sends the tags back — possibly more accurate, but slower and dependent on connectivity.\n\n---\n\n**Why this distinction matters today:**\n\nCompanies are increasingly blending the two approaches — sometimes preprocessing data on the edge to reduce bandwidth and then sending a lighter version to the cloud for advanced analytics. This hybrid approach is especially relevant in fields like healthcare wearables, autonomous vehicles, and smart manufacturing.\n\nSo in short: *Edge AI focuses on speed, privacy, and autonomy; Cloud AI on scale, capacity, and centralization.* The choice often depends on the specific constraints and priorities of the project.\n\nIf you’d like, I can share some examples of common edge AI tools and libraries you could experiment with on a small dev board — some of them are surprisingly beginner-friendly and fun for side projects.\n\n## User\n\nHey, could you help me make this email sound a bit more polished while keeping it friendly?\n\n---\n\nHi Sam,\n\nThanks for inviting me over this weekend! Every skater naturally develops a preferred 'regular' stance over time: **regular footed** (left foot forward) or **goofy footed** (right foot forward). This stance feels natural when pushing, rolling, and setting up for tricks.\n\nWhen you ride 'switch,' you essentially rotate your whole approach 180°, but still try to execute the trick as if it were in your normal stance. For onlookers (especially those not immersed in skate culture), this can look identical to the regular stance execution, because the shape of the trick—the path the board travels, the flick, the spin—remains visually similar. What’s different is the body mechanics: your balance, push leg, muscle memory, and timing cues are all flipped. This reversal makes it substantially more challenging, since you’re fighting years of ingrained habit.\n\nAnnouncers (especially in contest or video coverage) emphasize when a trick is done switch because:\n1. **Skill Recognition:** Switch tricks are harder and require extra precision.\n2. **Judging Criteria:** In competitions, variety and difficulty are scored; switch adds difficulty points.\n3. **Cultural Respect:** Within skateboarding culture, doing a trick well in switch demonstrates a deep level of board control and progression. Those who follow the scene value it as part of a skater’s style and versatility.\n\nThe reason it can look deceptively the same is that a skilled skater makes it *look easy*—their switch execution mirrors their regular stance tricks so well that the only giveaways are subtle foot position changes, push direction, or the initial stance roll‑up. Veteran viewers and announcers train their eyes to catch these details immediately.\n\nSo when you hear an announcer say 'switch backside flip' or 'switch nosegrind,' they’re signalling to the in‑the‑know audience: *Hey, that wasn’t just the trick—it was the trick in its hardest possible stance.*\n\n## User\n\nHow can someone who's new to watching skateboarding learn to spot when a trick is done in switch stance?\n\n## Assistant\n\nIf you’re just starting to watch skateboarding and want to spot switch stance, it helps to focus on a few key visual cues:\n\n1. **Push Foot Clues:** Most skaters push with their back foot in their natural stance, so when riding s",

In most cases, when you buy or mint a token representing an artwork, the blockchain holds two main pieces of on-chain data: the token’s ownership record and a pointer (often a URL) to where the artwork file actually resides.\n\nFor large media files — especially high-resolution images, animations, or interactive pieces — storing them *directly* on-chain is prohibitively expensive and technically inefficient. That’s why many projects use **off-chain storage**: the asset is placed on a traditional web server or cloud storage solution, and the token simply references it.\n\nThe problem: if that storage provider goes offline, the asset moves, or the hosting service isn’t maintained over the years, the link in the token will point to nothing. The blockchain proof of ownership will still exist — your token remains valid — but the content it represents may become inaccessible. In practical terms, your ability to display or verify the visual part of the artwork could be lost.\n\nCollectors, archivists, and creators try to mitigate this risk by using **decentralized storage systems** like **IPFS (InterPlanetary File System)** or **Arweave**. These distribute the file across many nodes, making it much harder for it to disappear as long as at least one node continues hosting it. Even then, long-term persistence depends on enough parties continuing to store and pin the file.\n\nFor truly permanent preservation, some projects store the entire artwork on-chain (possible for small files or generative code art) or ensure multiple redundant backups across independent organizations. This blends technical fault tolerance with cultural archiving — an important consideration, especially in art-focused blockchain projects.\n\nSo if the artwork file is hosted off-chain with no redundancy and the service vanishes, the visual experience of that token could effectively vanish too. This is why savvy buyers and creators often investigate where and how the files are stored before deciding to acquire or mint a piece.\n\n## User\n\nCan you help me refine this professional email for clarity and tone?\n\nHi Jamie,\n\nI hope you're doing well. This creates a whip-like motion that lifts the board upward and forward. Your planted foot nudges the deck into the desired direction or rotation before you jump back on. It’s a hybrid between a pop trick and a step trick — part of the execution happens *off* the board.\n\nWith a regular pop trick (like a kickflip or 360 flip), style points often come from the crispness of the pop, the height, and the control while airborne. In contrast, the 'no comply' leans heavily into **flow** and **body language** — the way your non-popping foot touches down changes your posture, making it look casual, improvisational, and almost dance-like.\n\n**Culturally**, 'no comply' tricks carry a strong association with creative, street-level skateboarding. They became a hallmark of skaters who favored crusty urban spots, curb cuts, and natural terrain over the polished predictability of skateparks. In the late ’80s and early ’90s, skaters like Natas Kaupas and later Ray Barbee used them to break away from the rigid contest format and express individuality. Today, they’re a go-to in modern skate edits for adding *texture* to a line.\n\nThey score higher *style points* not in the competitive sense, but in the respect they earn for taste and originality. A clean 'no comply' can feel almost like a well-timed line of improvisational jazz — subtle footwork, personal rhythm, and that sense of “I didn’t just land it, I *interpreted* it.”\n\nIn a way, if you compare it to coding, a regular pop trick is like writing technically flawless, performant code. While this smoother motion is most obvious in gaming, it can also affect comfort during prolonged use, particularly in peripheral vision where humans are more sensitive to motion artifacts. For static activities like coding, high refresh rates don’t directly make text clearer, but they can reduce subtle, subconscious strain caused by micro-movements — like scrolling through code or UI animations.\n\nAnother factor is that modern high-refresh monitors often use better backlighting and higher-quality panel electronics. For example, flicker-free backlight designs and higher PWM (pulse-width modulation) frequencies help minimize the rapid brightness fluctuations that can cause fatigue or discomfort for some users. This improved lighting stability can be more important for comfort than the raw refresh rate number.\n\nEye strain itself is multifactorial. Aside from refresh rate, factors include:\n- **Ambient lighting match**: Having screen brightness close to surrounding brightness levels.\n- **Contrast and text rendering**: Clear fonts and high-DPI displays reduce the effort your eyes need to focus.\n- **Blue light exposure**: While evidence for direct harm is debated, warmer color temperatures in the evening can subjectively reduce discomfort.\n- **Break routine**: Following the 20-20-20 rule (every 20 minutes, look at something 20 feet away for 20 seconds).\n\nInterestingly, many people who thought they were ‘immune’ to screen fatigue have noticed differences when switching between monitors with and without flicker control or with varying refresh rates. That may be why your comfort seems better lately — even if you’re not consciously aware of what changed.\n\n**Practical takeaway:** If you spend many hours coding daily, a high-refresh monitor that’s flicker-free and has good ergonomics (height, tilt, rotation) *can* make a noticeable difference, especially in long-term comfort. Could you explain exactly what that means and why it’s such a big deal when it comes up?\n\n## Assistant\n\nSure! In the context of skateboarding, 'NBD' stands for 'Never Been Done.' It’s shorthand that skaters, filmers, and skate media use to highlight a trick or line that, to anyone’s knowledge, has never been successfully landed before in that spot, in that way, or sometimes even at all.\n\nThe reason it’s a big deal comes down to skateboarding culture’s deep emphasis on creativity, progression, and originality. Landing an NBD isn’t just about technical skill — it’s about contributing something genuinely new to the collective history of skateboarding. Every famous skate spot, whether it’s ledges in Barcelona, a handrail in California, or a DIY bowl in some hidden warehouse, has a kind of 'roster' of what’s been done there. When someone pulls off an NBD, they’re expanding that shared record and, in a sense, leaving their mark on the cultural map.\n\nAn NBD can be:\n- **A completely new trick:** Inventing a maneuver no one has documented before (like Rodney Mullen introducing the kickflip back in the 1980s).\n- **A trick that’s been done, but in a totally new context:** For example, taking a standard trick and performing it over an especially notorious gap, or combining it with a manual or grind that changes its difficulty.\n- **A unique line:** Stringing together several moves in sequence in a way no one’s captured before at a recognizable spot.\n\nWhy it’s such a big deal is also tied to the documentation culture in skating. With decades of skate videos, magazine photos, and now Instagram clips creating an informal historical record, other skaters know instantly whether something’s been seen before. Calling something an NBD is almost like issuing a rare 'achievement unlocked' in gaming — the community collectively acknowledges it.\n\nAnd because skateboarding has no official governing body telling people what to do, NBDs help keep the scene alive by pushing its creative boundaries organically. There are quite a few factors that can shape why two breweries producing, say, an IPA or a porter can end up with noticeably different taste profiles, even within the same city. Here are the main elements at play:\n\n1. **Ingredient Sourcing and Terroir:** Much like coffee or wine, beer ingredients reflect the geography of where they’re grown. Local BC breweries might use hops from the Fraser Valley, the Yakima Valley in Washington, or experimental micro-hop farms on Vancouver Island. The specific hop variety — and even the year’s weather patterns — can change how citrusy, piney, or floral the resulting beer tastes. Similarly, malt from different roasters can impart anything from biscuit-like sweetness to deep caramel or chocolate undertones.\n\n2. **Water Chemistry:** Vancouver’s water is naturally soft (low in dissolved minerals), while other BC towns might have higher calcium or sulfate content. Small breweries often choose to either use raw local water or tweak mineral levels to suit a particular beer style. Even subtle differences in pH or mineral balance can emphasise bitterness, sweetness, or mouthfeel.\n\n3. **Yeast Strains:** Yeast does far more than just ferment sugars into alcohol — it creates esters and phenols that form much of beer’s aroma and flavour. Two breweries may use entirely different yeast strains for the same style, resulting in wildly different profiles: one batch might lean fruity or tropical, another more spicy or earthy.\n\n4. **Brewing Technique and Equipment:** Small differences in mash temperature, fermentation temperature, oxygen exposure, and filtration can alter flavour complexity and clarity. Craftspeople often have their own methodology or 'signature processes' — essentially a brewer’s fingerprint.\n\n5. **Batch Size and Freshness:** Small-batch brewing means more variability between releases, which can be part of its charm. Beers at a local tasting event are often consumed very fresh, intensifying hop aromas or other volatile flavour compounds that might fade in mass-produced equivalents.\n\n6. **Creative Additions:** Especially in BC’s craft scene, brewers experiment. Two versions of a pale ale could diverge simply because one uses locally foraged spruce tips or seasonal fruit during secondary fermentation.\n\nFrom a flavour science standpoint, it’s a mix of biology (yeast behaviour), chemistry (water and malt chemistry), and artistry (ingredient choice and brewing style). If you’re curious to dive deeper, you might explore resources like the BC Ale Trail’s brewery profiles, which sometimes list their water sources or preferred hop varieties — great for connecting tasting notes to brewing decisions.\n\nIf you’d like, I can also suggest some small-scale brewery tours in the Vancouver area where brewers are happy to walk you through their process in person.\n\n## User\n\nI’m planning a weekend trip to a small town I’ve never visited before—what kinds of interesting activities or local gatherings should I look out for to really get a feel for the community?\n\n## Assistant\n\nSince you enjoy local craft beer, see if the town has a microbrewery tour or a craft beer tasting event this weekend. Before aircraft-based cameras were practical (early 20th century), cartographers relied heavily on labor-intensive land surveys, triangulation, and artistic interpretation, which limited both accuracy and scale.\n\nOnce aerial photography became feasible — initially through balloons, kites, and later fixed-wing aircraft during World War I — it introduced three critical innovations:\n\n1. **Bird’s-Eye Perspective at High Resolution**: Aerial photographs allowed mapmakers to see large stretches of terrain in a single image, enabling detection of features like rivers, infrastructure, and terrain contours that were difficult or impossible to grasp from ground level.\n\n2. **Photogrammetry**: Engineers developed techniques to measure distances, elevations, and angles from overlapping aerial images. This allowed for the creation of topographic maps with unprecedented precision, and these principles directly inspired later methods used in satellite remote sensing and LiDAR mapping.\n\n3. **Time-Based Change Detection**: Aerial imagery made it possible to track changes over time — for example, the movement of coastlines, deforestation, urban growth, and post-disaster assessments. This temporal aspect became foundational in environmental monitoring, an approach still used in modern GIS (Geographic Information Systems) workflows.\n\nDuring World War II, the field expanded with the integration of stereoscopic photography — overlapping images viewed through stereoscopes allowed analysts to perceive depth and measure terrain elevations more accurately.
